"regulations provide for 6 or 7m² per person.971 A typical room in an initial reception centre has between 2\nand 4 beds, there are chairs and a table and each resident has a locker for themselves. Size of rooms\nmay vary, but rooms with a single bed are highly exceptional.\n\nMost initial reception centres have a policy to accommodate single women and families in separate\nbuildings or separate wings of their buildings. The AnKER centre in Manching/Ingolstadt for example\nprovides separate rooms for vulnerable persons.\n\nBath and toilet facilities usually consist of shower rooms and toilets which people have to share. As\nmentioned before, there are no regulations that oblige the Federal states to fulfil any standards. However,\nthe Federal Ministry for Family Affairs, Senior Citizens, Women and Youth and UNICEF have worked out\na policy paper stating minimum standards concerning the protection of refugees in reception centres.972\nThese guidelines state that sanitary facilities should be separate between genders and that those facilities\nshould be built in a way that guarantees privacy. Other guidelines recommend that one shower should be\navailable for 10 to 12 persons, but in some reception centres the ratio is worse than that, particularly in\nsituations of overcrowding. Cleaning of shared space (halls, corridors) as well as of sanitary facilities is\ncarried out by external companies in the initial reception centres.\n\nFood is supplied in the initial reception centres and is usually served in canteens on the premises of the\ncentres. In general, two or more menus are on offer for lunch and the management of the catering facilities\ntries to ensure that specific food is provided with regard to religious sentiments. Some, but not all initial\nreception centres also have shared kitchen space which enables asylum seekers to cook their own food;\nin AnkER centres, for instance, cooking is not allowed. Refrigerators for the use of asylum seekers are\navailable in some initial reception centres, but this seems to be the exception. In some centres, the\nmanagement does not allow hot water boilers for asylum seekers as this would be forbidden by fire\nregulations.973\n\nThe living conditions in many initial reception centres have been criticised by asylum seekers, volunteers\nand NGOs – especially in light of the extended obligatory stay in these facilities. In 2022 the conditions\ndeteriorated even more due to the massive overcrowding as consequence from the war in Ukraine and\nthe situation in Afghanistan. But still in 2023, small cities like Pulheim in North Rhine-Westphalia,974 as\nwell as middle size municipalities like Augsburg in Bavaria975 and Aachen in North Rhine-Westphalia976\nbut also big municipalities like Berlin977 and Hamburg978 face difficulties in accommodating new\nprotection seekers. In Berlin and Hamburg, around 97% percent of the reception capacities were\noccupied around October and November 2023.979\n\n\n\n\n971\n      European Migration Network, The Organisation of Reception Facilities for Asylum Seekers in Germany, 2013,\n      available in German at: https://bit.ly/48ojgO9, 26.\n972\n      Federal Ministry for Family Affairs, Senior Citizens, Women and Youth and UNICEF, Mindeststandards\n      zum Schutz von geflüchteten Menschen in Flüchtlingsunterkünften, available in German at:\n      https://bit.ly/48WAaV2.\n973\n      PROASYL and Refugee Council Berlin, Das Asylbewerberleistungsgesetz – Einschränkungen des\n      Grundrechts auf ein menschenwürdiges Existenzminimum für Geflüchtete, November 2022, available in\n      German at: https://bit.ly/3XrdSox, 100.\n974\n       WDR.de, Geflüchtete klagen über menschenunwürdige Zustände in Pulheimer Flüchtlingsunterkünften,\n      available at: https://bit.ly/3SKjme1.\n975\n      Augsburger Allgemeine, Augsburg gerät bei der Unterbringung von Geflüchteten wieder stärker unter Druck,\n      19 August 2023, available in German at: https://bit.ly/42MZ1Iq.\n976\n      InfoMigrants, ‘Refugees: German cities are reaching their limits’, 26 September 2022, available at:\n      https://bit.ly/3wpxjCp.\n977\n      Silke Mehring, Leichtbauhallen in Tegel geplant Unterbringung von Ukraine-Flüchtlingen in Berlin wird immer\n      komplizierter, rbb24, 02 December.2022, available in German at: http://bit.ly/3wdFPUT.\n978\n      Ibid.\n979\n      Rbb24.de, Berlin droht Verlust von tausenden Unterkunftsplätzen für Geflüchtete, 28 Novermber 2023,\n      available in German at: https://bit.ly/48nVWzN; NDR, Flüchtlingszahlen: Senat sieht Hamburg am Limit, 6\n      October 2023, available in German at: https://bit.ly/3SHfJ7w.\n\n\n                                                     151\n\nAccording to the administration of Berlin, 10,000 additional places were required in 2022.980 At the end\nof September 2023, the senate administration of Berlin spoke again of 8,000 more required places until\nthe end of 2023.981 Those necessary places are to be created in gymnasiums, hotels, exhibitions halls\nand through further usage of the old airport Tegel. In October 2023, the initial reception centre in Suhl,\nThuringia, was completely full and had imposed a freeze on admissions.982 However, the municipalities\ncannot offer any other accommodation facilities as all centres and apartments are full. The only possibility\nleft, according to the Thuringian Association of Towns and Municipalities, is the usage of sport halls.983\nMunicipalities in North Rine-Westphalia have started to rent hotels to provide accommodation for asylum\nseekers.984 Although this practice is very costly, it is preferred over blocking gymnasiums of local sport\nassociations. The authorities on the local, state and federal level blame each other for the shortcomings.\nWhile the local authorities are by law responsible for the accommodation of protection seekers,985 they\nclaim that the do not have enough financial and housing resources to fulfil the current need. They therefore\nask the Federal States to vacate more housing properties.986 The Federal States in turn urge the Federal\ngovernment to strengthen their efforts and to take up a coordinating role.987 After months of conflict, the\nFederal government and the Federal States have agreed in November 2023 – next to some deterrent\nmeasures that should decrease migration – upon more financial aid by the Federal Government.988\n\nAs consequence of the overcrowding, the authorities seem to be overburdened and deteriorating\nconditions have been reported. Under the law the state may derogate from the obligation to stay in initial\nreception centres in cases of overcrowding.989 Nevertheless, so far only Berlin has used this derogation\nclause and allows asylum seekers who have been allocated to Berlin under the “Köngisteiner Schlüssel”\nto live in private accommodations since the end of January 2023.990 In emergency shelters e.g. in Berlin,\nit has been reported that the tents at the former Berlin-Tegel airport do not protect from the cold causing\nnumerous illnesses and facilitating the spread of Covid-19 (on conditions in reception facilities during the\nCovid-19 pandemic see the 2021 Update).991 One partitioned area on such a tent is in general shared by\neight people. As a result, each asylum seeker has, according to calculations by the refugee council Berlin,\nonly about 2,6 m2 available for themselves.992 Additionally, since the airport is surrounded by barbed wire\nno systematic access for NGOs and volunteers is granted. At the same time protection seekers need to\ntake a shuttle bus to enter and exit the emergency shelter, thereby making it difficult for protection seekers\nto access legal aid and social assistance. In the emergency tents in Bremen protection seekers report of\nnon-functional and unclean sanitary facilities, coldness due to non-functional heating systems and a tense\natmosphere.993 Only one month later the municipality of Bremen decided to evacuate the tents due to the\n\n980\n      Thomas Frey, Ukraine-Ankunftszentrum wird um 3200 Plätze erweitert, Berliner Woche 06 January 2023,\n      available in German at: http://bit.ly/3CY9Jjy.\n981\n      Senatskanzlei Berlin, Mehr Plätze für die Unterbringung von Geflüchteten, press release from 26 September\n      2023, available in German at: https://bit.ly/3wnKSpd.\n982\n      Mdr.de, Erstaufnahmeeinrichtungen für Flüchtlinge – Was tun, wenn sie voll sind?, 6 October 2023, available\n      in German at: https://bit.ly/3I9iV74.\n983\n      Ibid.\n984\n      Wdr.de, Flüchtlinge in Kommunen: Letzter Ausweg Hotel, 26 January 2023, available in German at:\n      https://bit.ly/3UPlLFY.\n985\n      §44 AsylG in conjunction with the different Federal state’s Reception laws: e.g. §1 Landesaufnahmegesetz\n      Hesse; Inhabitants were partially relocated to emergency shelters on\nexhibition grounds. This solution was only temporary and local authorities have organised lightweight\nbuilding constructions which should serve until mid-2024.994 Even if asylum seekers do not live in tents\nbut in houses, the living conditions are in many cases catastrophic. In a reception centre in Pulheim,\nNorth Rhine-Westphalia, for example, the building smells heavily, the sanitary facilities are mouldy and\nrats run around the complex.995\n\nIn arrival centres, the overcrowding mostly leads to backlogs in the registration procedure and conflicts\namong the protection seekers stemming from the lack of privacy. Asylum seekers at the arrival centre in\nHamburg-Rahlstedt, for example, have reported inter alia a backlog of registration,996 lack of privacy,\nunclean sanitary facilities and disturbances at night. The sleeping areas are placed in former warehouses\nand divided by thin partitions into several compartments, which do not allow for privacy. Besides reading\nlamps attached to each bed, there is one common light for the whole warehouse, which is switched on\nfrom 8:00am to 22:00pm.997 A backlog of registration, lack of access to health care and social assistance\nhas been reported also from the arrival centre in Berlin.998 In the arrival centre in Thuringia, many violent\nconflicts have been reported stemming from the lack of staff members, stressed social workers and non-\ntrained security personnel.999 The backlog of registrations all over Germany has supposedly been caught\nup until the end of June 2023, according to the Federal Government.1000\n\nMore generally, studies published in 2020 have come to the conclusion that the accommodation in initial\nreception centres infringes upon children’s’ rights and constitutes a danger to their mental health. The\nspatial confinement, the experience of violence and removals, as well as the permanent uncertainty cause\npsychological stress and have a negative impact on children.1001 Health care and psychosocial support\nprovided for young refugees in the mass accommodations were described as worryingly inadequate for\nmost of the facilities.1002 The study of PROASYL and the Refugee Council Berlin support these findings.\nAccording to the study of 2022, especially the access to health care, access to adequate hygienic and\nother products such as strollers for toddlers are scarce.1003\n\nThe NGO ‘Ärzte der Welt’ (Doctors of the World) announced in September 2019 that an advice service\nrun by the organisation in the AnkER-centre of Manching/Ingolstadt was to be terminated. The NGO\ndescribed living conditions in the facility as ‘morbid’ and claimed that adequate treatment, in particular\ntreatment of persons with psychological disorders, was impossible under the circumstances. Insufficient\nprotection against assaults, lack of privacy and nocturnal disturbances were impeding the mental\nstabilisation of asylum-seekers at the facility and the NGO was no longer capable to bear responsibility\nfor the mental health of its patients. Moreover, the organisation claims that there was no system for the\nidentification of vulnerable persons in place at the facility.1004\n\n994\n       Weserreport, Geflüchtete ziehen um, 10 January "end_unit": 176, "locator": "page 175 through page 176", "exact_excerpt": "Legal changes as a result of the ‘enforcement deficit’ debate\n\nDespite the stable number of removals over the last years prior to the Covid-19 outbreak, an alleged\n‘enforcement deficit’ had become the subject of a heated political debate and a ‘media obsession’ in 2017\nand 2018, as the authorities were being criticised for their failure to carry out removals.1158 This debate\nhas continued under the government that took office in 2021, which pledged to increase efforts to enforce\nreturns.1159 The debate has led to numerous restrictive reforms in 2017, 2019, 2020 and 2022 as well to\nan additional reform proposed in November 2023 (see below), and to a demand for increased use of\ndetention in the removal procedure. In 2023, a total of 31,770 removals failed, compared to 16,430\neffective removals.1160 This does not mean that all 31,770 persons were not returned, however, since\nauthorities often carry out another removal attempt after the failed one.1161 The reasons for failure to carry\nout removals were as follows:\n\n                               Failed removals in focus: 2023 (whole year)\n The Federal Government has no information on the number of cases\nin which persons to be deported were responsible for the cancellations (e.g. by absconding) and there\nare numerous other possible reasons for the cancellation of removal attempts (such as medical reasons,\norganisational problems etc.). Since removals are not announced to the persons concerned, it is also\nlikely that persons can simply not be found on the date of the scheduled removals, due to them staying\nat another place rather than because they are deliberately avoiding arrest.1162 Nevertheless, despite the\nlack of empirical evidence, the comparatively high number of cancellations of removal attempts is often\nassociated with the absconding of the persons concerned.1163\n\nStatistics on removals from detention also show that an increase in detention is not necessarily associated\nwith a higher number of removals.1164 In addition, there are strong differences between the Federal States\nin how often detention actually results in a removal: by way of example, in North Rhine Westphalia and\nRhineland Palatinate, four out of five people detained in the first half of 2023 were also removed, while\nthe ration is only one out of ten in Saxony.1165 Nonetheless, over the past years, requests for a more\nfrequent use of detention pending removal in the political debate resulted in several legislative reforms\nsince 2015, of which the ones adopted over the past four years are briefly presented here.1166 August\n2019 saw the entry into force of the Second Act for an improved enforcement of the obligation to leave\nthe country (Zweites Gesetz zur besseren Durchsetzung der Ausreisepflicht, also known as the ‘Orderly\nReturn Act’/Geordnete-Rückkehr-Gesetz). The law expanded authorities’ power to access private\napartments and to arrest persons to be removed, expanded the grounds for detention and introduced a\nnew form of ‘detention to enforce the obligation to cooperate’ with authorities (Mitwirkungshaft. Section\n62 VI Residence Act; for details see the 2019 to the AIDA Country Report for Germany).1167\n\nThe numerous and increasingly restrictive legal changes in previous years continued with a new detention\nprovision in Section 62c Residence Act adopted in November 2020 and an extension of detention\npossibilities for criminal offenders which entered into force on 31 December 2022 (see Grounds for\ndetention). In October 2023, the Federal Government issued a proposal for an additional reform of\ndetention and the return procedure in the Act on the Improvement of Removals\n(Rückführungsverbesserungsgesetz).1168 The bill, which inter alia would expand detention grounds and\nduration, was voted on in parliament on 18 January 2024 but has not entered into force as of end February\n2024 (see Legal framework of detention).1169\n\n\n\n\n1162\n       Brief analysis of Dr. Thomas Hohlfeld (assistant to the parliamentary group of The Left) of the Federal\n       Governments reply 19/17100, 20 March 2020, available in German at: https://bit.ly/4aqLV7g, 5.\n1163\n       See for example DIE ZEIT; Mehr als jede zweite Abschiebung gescheitert, 24 February 2019, available in\n       German at: http://bit.ly/3wobZ06.\n1164\n       Mediendienst Integration, \"Im großen Stil\" abschieben?, 23 October 2023, available in German at:\n       https://bit.ly/3tPuZqp and Victoria Rietig and Mona Lou Günnewig, Deutsche Rückkehrpolitik und\n       Abschiebungen. 2023 Update", "publisher": "ECRE / AIDA", "raw_sha256": "35a16fff9b7b255d989c2a5cc6e56b8de7d67ce65e07a782935d6cac97244469", "fact_summary": "By 12 December 2023, 12,067 visas had been issued to family members of beneficiaries of subsidiary protection, fulfilling the annual quota of 12,000; this exceeded the 8,900 visas issued in 2022.", "question_ids": ["6B.2", "6B.3", "8B.6", "8B.7"], "polarity": "favorable", "period_fit": "Directly reports the 2023 outcome.", "ruler_attribution": "The outcome concerns federal and local administrative implementation during Scholz's government, without evidence of personal Chancellor direction.", "limitations": ["Meeting the quota did not remove the underlying discretionary legal framework or procedural barriers."], "start_unit": 223, "end_unit": 223, "locator": "page 223", "exact_excerpt": "This is regulated in Section 36a of the Residence Act, according to which only members of the ‘immediate\nfamily’ (spouses, registered partners, minor unmarried children, parents of unaccompanied children) are\neligible for family reunification. In order to be included in the monthly quota of 1,000 visa, ‘humanitarian\nreasons’ shall be decisive, which are listed in the law as follows:\n     v Long duration of separation of family members,\n     v Separation of families with at least one (minor) unmarried child,\n     v Serious risks to life, limb or personal freedom of a family member living abroad,\n     v Serious illness, need for care or serious disabilities of a family member living abroad.\n\nIn addition, the welfare of the child and ‘integration aspects’ (e.g., language skills, ability to provide for\nmeans of living) may be taken into account.1480\n\nThe monthly quota for visa has not been reached since the introduction of the new regulation for\nbeneficiaries of subsidiary protection, due to a complicated procedure involving three different authorities:\nEmbassies or consulates – often in cooperation with IOM – have to carry out an interview with the family\nmembers who have applied for visa; then the local alien’s offices in Germany have to decide whether the\nnecessary humanitarian criteria are fulfilled; and then they have to pass on the visa applications to the\nFederal Administrative Office (Bundesverwaltungsamt) which theoretically should select the most urgent\n1,000 cases per month.1481 In practice, this selection does not take place since procedures at the local\nauthorities are lengthy, resulting in less than 1,000 applications per month. As a result, the Federal\nAdministrative Office usually authorises all cases submitted by the local authorities and informs the\nembassies or consulates that visas may be issued.\n\nUntil 12 December 2023, 12,067 visas were issued to family members of beneficiaries of subsidiary\nprotection.1482 In contrast to the time between August 2018 and April 2021, where only 20,600 visas were\ngranted(62% of the total of 33,000 visas that the law would have foreseen for this period), the quota of\n12,000 per annum is thus fulfilled in 2023.1483 In 2022, 8,900 visas were issued, i.e., 74% of the quota of\n12,000 per year.1484 Around 17,594 requests for appointments at embassies were pending as of June\n2022.1485 Since it is likely that many persons have asked for appointments several times, the actual\nnumber of persons applying for visa for this purpose is likely to be lower.1486 10,778 of those visas in 2023\nwere granted to Syrian nationals, 176 to Afghan, 54 to Turkish, 83 to Iraqi and 2 to Iranian nationals.1487\n\nAlso for beneficiaries of subsidiary protection, the question arises concerning the time of their minority in\nrelation to family reunification. As already mentioned, the German Courts have in the past evaluated the\nage of the beneficiary at the time of the court decision and must now refer to the time of the asylum\napplication due to several CJEU rulings (see above). However, those rulings are based on the directive\n2003/86/EC,1488 which does not apply to beneficiaries of subsidiary protection.1489 Thus, German courts\nhave argued, that the right to family reunification ends when the subsidiary protection status holder\n\n1480\n       Detailed information on the legal requirements and the procedure can be found at: https://familie.asyl.net/.\n1481\n "770fdef8db2bdcec4a4eb01b68c41b65a693fb9c2ea46dcf06c6d2e90e348579", "verification_status": "accepted", "verification_notes": []}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0014-E007", "source_id": "SRC-e9dabb799fde21e4", "url": "https://www.bundesregierung.de/breg-de/aktuelles/nationale-sicherheitsstrategie-2195890", "title": "Nationale Sicherheitsstrategie vorgestellt Integrierte Sicherheit für Deutschland", "publisher": "Bundesregierung", "raw_sha256": "b9235a2907b45e6f0644e276fa90631e84146f258e483862260f167ca6a9a4ea", "fact_summary": "On 14 June 2023, Scholz's cabinet adopted Germany's first National Security Strategy. It established an integrated-security approach covering defense, civil protection, diplomacy, policing, development, cyber-security, supply-chain resilience, energy and raw-material diversification, climate risks, biodiversity, food security, and pandemic prevention; Scholz described the strategy as the starting point for a continuous cross-government process.", "question_ids": ["1B.10", "1B.9", "3B.9", "5B.9", "6B.6", "8B.1", "8B.2", "8B.5", "8B.6", "8B.8"], "polarity": "favorable", "period_fit": "Directly within 2023-2023.", "ruler_attribution": "Scholz presented the cabinet-adopted strategy with four ministers and described its purpose and continuing implementation process.", "limitations": ["The source documents adoption and stated institutional design, but not completed implementation, funding adequacy, or measurable outcomes by year-end."], "start_unit": 6, "end_unit": 9, "locator": "extracted HTML block 6 through extracted HTML block 9", "exact_excerpt": "Zum Mastodon-Kanal der Bundesregierung\nZum YouTube-Kanal der Bundesregierung\nNewsletter-Anmeldung der Bundesregierung\nFolgen Sie dem Bundeskanzler:\nZum Instagram-Account des Bundeskanzlers\nZum TikTok-Kanal des Bundeskanzlers\nZum X-Kanal des Bundeskanzlers\nKontakt\nErklärung zur Barrierefreiheit\nBarriere melden\nGebärdensprache\nLeichte Sprache\nNavigations-Hinweise\nEnglish\nFrançais\nReformen für Deutschland\nAktuelles\nSchwerpunkte\nBundeskanzler\nService\nAktuelles\nNationale Sicherheitsstrategie vorgestellt\nIntegrierte Sicherheit für Deutschland\nEin verändertes sicherheitspolitisches Umfeld, eine zunehmende Multipolarität, Bedrohungen für die Demokratie, Gesellschaft und Wirtschaft und die Auswirkungen der Klimakrise. Das Kabinett hat die Nationale Sicherheitsstrategie beschlossen – als Antwort auf diese Herausforderungen und zum Schutz Deutschlands und seiner Bürgerinnen und Bürger.\nMittwoch, 14. Juni 2023\n\n4 Min. Lesedauer\nper E-Mail teilen, Integrierte Sicherheit für Deutschland\nper Facebook teilen, Integrierte Sicherheit für Deutschland\nBeitrag teilen\nE-Mail\nper E-Mail teilen, Integrierte Sicherheit für Deutschland\nFacebook\nper Facebook teilen, Integrierte Sicherheit für Deutschland\nThreema\nper Threema teilen, Integrierte Sicherheit für Deutschland\nWhatsApp\nper Whatsapp teilen, Integrierte Sicherheit für Deutschland\nX\nper X teilen, Integrierte Sicherheit für Deutschland\nLink kopieren\nDie Nationale Sicherheitsstrategie beantwortet viele Fragen: Wer bedroht unser Land und wie? Und wer kümmert sich darum das zu verhindern?\nGrafik: Bundesregierung\n„Zum ersten Mal in der Geschichte unseres Landes haben wir eine Nationale Sicherheitsstrategie für die Bundesrepublik Deutschland erarbeitet“, sagte Bundeskanzler Olaf Scholz. Gemeinsam mit Außenministerin Annalena Baerbock, Bundesfinanzminister Christian Lindner, Bundesinnenministerin Nancy Faeser und Verteidigungsminister Boris Pistorius stellte der Kanzler nach der Kabinettssitzung die nun beschlossene Strategie vor.\nStark veränderte Rahmenbedingungen\n„Was sich früher als Weißbuch allein auf die Verteidigungspolitik beschränkte, folgt nun einem viel umfassenderen, systematischen Gesamtansatz“, so Scholz weiter. Bereits im Koalitionsvertrag der regierungsbildenden Parteien enthalten, sei die Erarbeitung der Nationalen Sicherheitsstrategie in den vergangenen 18 Monaten mit massiven sicherheitspolitischen Rahmenbedingungen konfrontiert und von ihnen beeinflusst worden.\nDer Auftrag zur Erarbeitung ging an das Auswärtige Amt – „vor der Zeitenwende, vor dem russischen Überfall auf die Ukraine, vor dem brutalen Angriffskrieg, der seither in der Ukraine tobt, und vor den Explosionen der Nordstream Pipelines“, so der Kanzler. Bei allen Veränderungen bleibe es die zentrale Aufgabe des Staates, ohne Abstriche für die Sicherheit seiner Bürgerinnen und Bürger zu sorgen. „Denn: Ohne Sicherheit gibt es keine Freiheit, keine Stabilität und auch keinen Wohlstand.“\nAußenministerin Baerbock sagte, der russische Angriffskrieg gegen die Ukraine habe gezeigt, „dass Frieden und Freiheit nicht vom Himmel fallen“. Die Herausforderungen für die Sicherheit Deutschlands zögen sich durch alle Lebensbereiche. Dies gelte etwa für Medikamente genauso wie für den\nCyber\n-Raum oder die Sauberkeit von Wasser.\nDie Sicherheit Deutschlands brauche eine „360-Grad-Perspektive“, sagte Bundesfinanzminister Lindner. Dazu leisten alle Ressorts der Bundesregierung einen Beitrag. Auch die Finanzpolitik sei hier gefordert, so Lindner. Die Corona-Pandemie und der Energiepreisschock des vergangenen Jahres wären unter anderen Umständen mit einer erheblichen Gefährdung des sozialen Friedens verbunden gewesen, „wenn wir nicht über die fiskalischen Reserven verfügt hätten“. Zum anderen hänge mit der internationalen Finanzstabilität unmittelbar Krisenprävention zusammen.\n\nDrei zentrale Dimensionen des politischen Handelns\n„Wehrhaft. Resilient. Nachhaltig. Integrierte Sicherheit für Deutschland“ – unter diesem Titel steht die Nationale Sicherheitsstrategie.\nWehrhaftigkeit\nOberste Aufgabe der Sicherheitspolitik ist sicherzustellen, dass die Bürgerinnen und Bürger in Deutschland auch künftig in Frieden, in Freiheit und Sicherheit leben können. Dafür muss Deutschland sich und seine Verbündeten schützen und verteidigen können.\nDazu bekennt sich die Bundesregierung weiter unverrückbar zu\nNATO\nund\nEU\n. Die Bundeswehr wird nachhaltig gestärkt, um ihren Kernauftrag – die Landes- und Bündnisverteidigung – zu gewährleisten. Durch einen gesamtgesellschaftlichen Ansatz werden die Zivilverteidigung und der Bevölkerungsschutz gestärkt.\nUnverändert bekennt sich die Bundesregierung zur Verantwortung im internationalen Krisenmanagement. Denn die Sicherheit Deutschlands ist untrennbar verbunden mit der Sicherheit und Stabilität anderer Weltregionen.\nResilienz\nEin Leben in Freiheit kann nur gewährleistet werden, wenn Gesellschaft und Volkswirtschaft widerstandsfähig, anpassungsfähig und im Inneren gefestigt sind. Deshalb muss die freiheitlich-demokratische Grundordnung gegen illegitime Einflussnahme von außen geschützt und verteidigt werden.\nGemeinsam mit den internationalen Partnern tritt die Bundesregierung ein für eine freie internationale Ordnung auf Grundlage des Völkerrechts und der Charta der Vereinten Nationen. Ziel ist es auch, einseitige Abhängigkeiten in der Rohstoff- und Energieversorgung durch Diversifizierung der Lieferbeziehung zu reduzieren.\nDie Bundesregierung wird Rohstoffprojekte gemeinsam mit der Wirtschaft gezielt fördern und strategische Reserven anlegen. Außerdem wird sie die Cyber-Sicherheit stärken und auch der Weltraumsicherheit mehr Aufmerksamkeit widmen.\nNachhaltigkeit\nEine fundamentale Bedrohung – insbesondere für die kommenden Generationen – erwächst aus den Folgen der Klimakrise und der Bedrohung der natürlichen Lebensgrundlagen.\nNicht nur bei der Bekämpfung der Klima-, Biodiversität- und Ökosystemkrise, der Stärkung der globalen Ernährungssicherheit und der globalen Pandemieprävention gilt daher für die Bundesregierung das Primat der Prävention: frühzeitiges Handeln rettet Leben.\nWeitere Informationen finden Sie auch auf der Website zur\nNationalen Sicherheitstrategie\n. Das Dokument im Wortlaut finden Sie hier\nzum Nachlesen\n.\nKanzler Scholz (2.v.l.) und die Ministerinnen und Minister Lindner, Baerbock, Faeser und Pistorius (v.l.) auf dem Weg in die Bundespressekonferenz.\nFoto: Bundesregierung/Denzel\nIntegrierte Sicherheit ist mehr als Verteidigung\n\nDie Nationale Sicherheitsstrategie zieht Schlüsse für die Zukunft, um die Sicherheit zu gewährleisten. Dafür begründet sie eine Politik der Integrierten Sicherheit. „Es geht nicht allein um Verteidigung und Bundeswehr“, erläuterte Kanzler Scholz. Es gehe vielmehr „um die ganze Palette unserer Sicherheit – über Diplomatie genauso wie Polizei, Feuerwehr und Technische Hilfswerke, über die Entwicklungs-Zusammenarbeit, über\nCyber\n-Sicherheit und über die Resilienz von Lieferketten.“\nDie Strategie, so Scholz, sei nicht „Endpunkt, sondern Ausgangspunkt“ für einen kontinuierlichen Prozess, bei dem alle staatlichen Ebenen für eine dauerhafte Stärkung der Sicherheit zusammenwirken – aber auch Wirtschaft und Gesellschaft. „Deutschland ist ein starkes Land. Deshalb können wir die Aufgaben, die vor uns liegen, voller Zuversicht und Vertrauen angehen.“\nDie Nationale Sicherheitsstrategie, so Außenministerin Baerbock, richtet sich nicht nur an die Bundesregierung, sondern an die gesamte Gesellschaft. „Die Sicherheitsstrategie wird nur funktionieren, wenn wir sie europäisch und transatlantisch verankern“. Nötig sei eine viel engere Zusammenarbeit mit Partnern auf anderen Kontinenten.\nNach der Nationalen Sicherheitsstrategie hat die Bundesregierung am 13. Juli 2023 erstmals eine ressortübergreifende und umfassende\nChina-Strategie\nbeschlossen. Zentrales Ziel der Strategie ist es, den komplexen Beziehungen zu China eine Struktur und eine Richtung zu geben – in vollem Einklang mit den Zielsetzungen der\nEU\n.\nBeitrag teilen\nper E-Mail teilen, Integrierte Sicherheit für Deutschland\nper Facebook teilen, Integrierte Sicherheit für Deutschland\nper Threema teilen, Integrierte Sicherheit für Deutschland\nBeitrag teilen\nE-Mail\nper E-Mail teilen, Integrierte Sicherheit für Deutschland\nFacebook\nper Facebook teilen, Integrierte Sicherheit für Deutschland\nThreema\nper Threema teilen, Integrierte Sicherheit für Deutschland\nWhatsApp\nper Whatsapp teilen, Integrierte Sicherheit für Deutschland\nX\nper X teilen, Integrierte Sicherheit für Deutschland", "excerpt_sha256": "b4d24b4b02b51822a143ce272c8604416a84fb993b4a5c869fa1f07ba0125cfa", "verification_status": "accepted", "verification_notes": []}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0014-E008", "source_id": "SRC-0df39423b793e358", "url": "https://www.bundesregierung.de/breg-de/service/newsletter-und-abos/bulletin/rede-von-bundeskanzler-olaf-scholz-2233046", "title": "Rede von Bundeskanzler Olaf Scholz beim 6. deutsch-ukrainischen Wirtschaftsforum am 24. Oktober 2023 in Berlin", "publisher": "Federal Government of Germany", "raw_sha256": "b3eeb26849ea473b241bb7a58ab4160e7a3f1ace2d972d2082bf6b53ed3b55db", "fact_summary": "At the 24 October 2023 German-Ukrainian Economic Forum, Scholz stated that Germany's civilian and military aid to Ukraine since the start of the war totaled €24 billion, announced a €1.4 billion winter air-defense package, reported nearly €80 million for repairing and improving Ukraine's electricity network, and described plans for EU grants, loans, investment coordination, and reform-linked reconstruction support.", "question_ids": ["2B.5", "2B.8", "5B.6", "6B.6", "8B.10", "8B.2", "8B.3", "8B.6", "8B.9"], "polarity": "mixed", "period_fit": "Directly within 2023-2023.", "ruler_attribution": "Scholz reported German government commitments and advocated EU-level reconstruction mechanisms.", "limitations": ["The speech is a government account and does not independently verify the amounts or assess outcomes.", "The military-support facts bear on external war policy, not a German-initiated war."], "start_unit": 7, "end_unit": 8, "locator": "extracted HTML block 7 through extracted HTML block 8", "exact_excerpt": "Threema\nper Threema teilen, Rede von Bundeskanzler Olaf Scholz\nWhatsApp\nper Whatsapp teilen, Rede von Bundeskanzler Olaf Scholz\nX\nper X teilen, Rede von Bundeskanzler Olaf Scholz\nLink kopieren\nSehr geehrter Herr Staatspräsident Selensky,\nsehr geehrter Herr Premierminister Schmyhal,\nsehr geehrter Herr Adrian,\nsehr geehrter Herr Bruch,\nmeine Damen und Herren!\nEs war mir wichtig, auch in diesem Jahr beim deutsch-ukrainischen Wirtschaftsforum zu sprechen. Seit mehr als 600 Tagen wehrt sich die Ukraine heldenhaft gegen einen von Russland begonnenen, verbrecherischen Angriffskrieg, der auf nichts weniger als die Vernichtung der ukrainischen Staatlichkeit gerichtet ist. Dieser Angriff ist zugleich auch ein Angriff auf das grundlegende Prinzip der europäischen und internationalen Friedensordnung, nämlich dass Grenzen nicht mit Gewalt verschoben werden dürfen. Auch deshalb darf Russland mit seinen imperialistischen Motiven nicht durchkommen. Auch deshalb unterstützen wir die Ukraine wirtschaftlich, finanziell, humanitär und auch mit Waffen.\nDiese Unterstützung wird in keiner Weise dadurch beeinträchtigt, dass wir seit den schrecklichen Morgenstunden des 7. Oktober natürlich zugleich mit größter Anteilnahme und in größter Sorge nach Israel und in den Nahen Osten schauen. Beiden Ländern – Israel und der Ukraine – gilt unsere unverbrüchliche Solidarität.\nPutin hat die Ukraine überfallen, weil er in imperialistischer Verblendung mit einem einfachen und schnellen Sieg rechnete. Er hat sich geirrt. Und er irrt sich erneut, wenn er glaubt, länger durchzuhalten als wir. Die Ukraine wird nicht aufhören, für ihre Freiheit zu kämpfen. Das ist in diesen mehr als 600 Tagen vollkommen unmissverständlich klar geworden.\nUnsere Zusage gilt: Wir unterstützen die Ukraine – so lange wie nötig! Zusammen mit unseren Partnerinnen und Partnern aus der EU, der G7 und vielen anderen Ländern lassen wir keinen Zweifel an unserer Entschlossenheit und unserer Durchhaltefähigkeit aufkommen. Unsere zivile und militärische Hilfe beläuft sich seit Kriegsbeginn auf 24 Milliarden Euro. Damit ist Deutschland hinter den USA der zweitgrößte Unterstützer der Ukraine.\nIm aufziehenden Winter spannen wir einen Schutzschirm gegen neuerliche russische Angriffe auf die Energie-, Wasser- und Wärmeinfrastruktur auf. Denn es zeichnet sich ab, dass Russland erneut Kälte und Energieknappheit als Waffe gegen die Zivilbevölkerung einsetzen will.\nUm den Himmel über der Ukraine gegen russische Drohnen und Raketen zu sichern, haben wir ein Winterpaket im Wert von noch einmal 1,4 Milliarden Euro geschnürt. Es enthält unter anderem ein weiteres Patriot-System, ein weiteres Iris-T-System sowie weitere Gepard-Flakpanzer mit neu dafür hergestellter Munition.\nJede Fabrik, die nicht zerstört wird, jede Stromleitung, die geschützt werden kann, jedes Unternehmen, in dem weiter produziert wird, hilft schließlich dabei, die Ukraine auch wirtschaftlich zu stabilisieren, und die Ukraine ist da auf einem guten Weg, Herr Premierminister. Drei Prozent Wirtschaftswachstum in diesem Jahr, robuste Prognosen auch für 2024 – das zeigt: Die Ukraine ist „open for business“, auch in diesen Zeiten, und wir unterstützen sie dabei, indem wir helfen, Kriegsschäden zu verhindern und zu beseitigen, und indem wir bereits seit dem vergangenen Jahr den Wiederaufbau unterstützen – nach dem Motto „Build back better!“.\nErst vergangene Woche haben wir einen Zuschuss von knapp 80 Millionen Euro auf den Weg gebracht, mit dem der ukrainische Energieversorger Kriegsschäden im Stromnetz beheben und gleichzeitig die Energieeffizienz des Gesamtnetzes verbessern kann, und w. Wenn man weiterdenkt, etwa an das große Potenzial, das die Ukraine im Bereich der erneuerbaren Energien hat, dann sind das echte Zukunftsinvestitionen.\nPotenzial hat die Ukraine übrigens nicht nur im Energiebereich, etwa beim Wasserstoff, sondern genauso in der Zulieferindustrie, im Agrarbereich, im IT-Sektor und bei kritischen Rohstoffen. Sie haben das erwähnt, Herr Ministerpräsident, und die Konferenz hier in Berlin unterstreicht das. Es ist sicher kein Zufall, dass trotz des Krieges mehr als 2.000 deutsche Unternehmen in der Ukraine aktiv sind – mit mehr als 35.000 Angestellten allein bei Zulieferern im Automobilsektor.\nWer heute in die Ukraine investiert, der investiert in ein künftiges EU-Mitgliedsland, das Teil unserer Rechtsgemeinschaft und unseres Binnenmarkts sein wird. Wie schnell der Beitrittsprozess geht, hängt von den Reformen ab, die die ukrainische Regierung unternimmt. Klar ist: Jede dieser Reformen wird das Investitionsklima weiter verbessern. Und klar ist auch: Wir werden die Ukraine auf ihrem Weg in die EU begleiten und auch bei den Reformen mit aller Kraft unterstützen.\nUm Investitionen in der Ukraine bereits jetzt zu erleichtern, haben wir in enger Rücksprache mit der Wirtschaft und Industrie noch einmal Verbesserungen bei den verfügbaren Garantieinstrumenten erreicht: bei der Höhe der Garantieübernahme, bei den Antragsgebühren und auch beim bürokratischen Aufwand. Damit federn wir bestehende Risiken ab, wobei die Wirtschaft natürlich am Ende von Ihnen, von den Unternehmerinnen und Unternehmern, gemacht wird.\nDeshalb ist es ein gutes Signal, sehr geehrter Herr Adrian, dass die Auslandshandelskammer mit eigenen Programmen deutsche Unternehmen in der Ukraine vernetzt, die sich in den unterschiedlichen Sektoren am Wiederaufbau beteiligen wollen. Die Privatwirtschaft wird bei dieser Mammutaufgabe Wiederaufbau eine ganz zentrale Rolle spielen. Und ich bin sicher: Auf die Expertise und das Engagement der deutschen Industrie ist dabei Verlass.\nWir als Bundesregierung sorgen dabei für den nötigen politischen Rahmen. Schon beim Europäischen Rat Ende dieser Woche werden wir unseren gemeinsamen Willen zur finanziellen Unterstützung der Ukraine bekräftigen. Und ich werde mich dafür einsetzen, dass wir bis Ende des Jahres die konkreten Lösungen auf den Weg bringen, um eine nachhaltige Unterstützung der Ukraine für die kommenden Jahre zu sichern.\n\nIn der EU wollen wir dafür eine Ukraine-Fazilität schaffen, die Zuschüsse und Darlehen bündelt, private und öffentliche Investitionen koordiniert und in der auch die technischen und weiteren unterstützenden Maßnahmen zusammengefasst werden, die wir als Europäische Union für Beitrittskandidaten zur Verfügung stellen.\nRichtschnur für diesen Wiederaufbau aus einem Guss soll ein von der Ukraine selbst erarbeiteter Plan sein, der mit der EU und auch international gut abgestimmt ist. Finanzielle Unterstützung wird dort auch mit der Erfüllung wichtiger Reformschritte verbunden. Bei der Abstimmung und der Erarbeitung von Reformschwerpunkten mit Partnern über die EU hinaus kommt auch der internationalen Geberplattform, die ich während der deutschen G7-Präsidentschaft im vergangenen Jahr ins Leben gerufen habe, wieder und weiter eine wichtige Rolle zu.\nIm Juni des kommenden Jahres werden wir zudem die internationalen Unterstützer der Ukraine – gemeinsam mit Ihrer Regierung, Herr Premierminister – zur Ukraine Recovery Conference 2024 nach Berlin einladen. Ziel ist es auch dort, der Ukraine langfristige wirtschaftliche und finanzielle Unterstützung zu sichern – sowohl durch die beteiligten Staaten als auch durch den Privatsektor. Dabei denken wir den Beitrittsprozess der Ukraine zur EU immer schon mit.\nAuf einen Aspekt möchte ich zum Schluss noch eingehen, weil er aus meiner Sicht der Garant dafür ist, dass sich die Wirtschaftsbeziehungen zwischen Deutschland und der Ukraine in Zukunft noch besser, noch dynamischer entwickeln. Neben der EU-Integration der Ukraine ist das der Umstand, dass fast eine Million Ukrainerinnen und Ukrainer inzwischen hier in Deutschland leben und arbeiten. Viele von ihnen beenden in diesen Wochen und Monaten ihre Integrationskurse. Viele sprechen inzwischen Deutsch. Fast alle sind gut ausgebildet.\nDen deutschen Unternehmerinnen und Unternehmern hier, die oft unter einem Mangel an Arbeitskräften leiden, möchte ich daher einen Rat geben: Nutzen Sie dieses große Potenzial! Integrieren Sie die Ukrainerinnen und Ukrainer, die hier bei uns sind, in Ihre Unternehmen! Selbst wenn diese Frauen und Männer nach dem Krieg zurück in die Ukraine gehen, sind sie für Ihre Unternehmen ein Gewinn, weil sie dann menschliche Brücken bilden in ein Land, das als EU-Beitrittskandidat enormes Wirtschaftspotenzial birgt: ein Land mit Millionen fleißigen, gut ausgebildeten Bürgerinnen und Bürgern; ein Land, mit dem wir – auch dank der Ukrainerinnen und Ukrainer, die derzeit hier bei uns leben – enger verbunden sind, als jemals zuvor.\nLieber Denys Schmyhal, liebe Teilnehmerinnen und Teilnehmer aus der Ukraine, die Aufgabe, vor der Sie stehen, ist groß. Sie erfüllen sie, während Ihr Land mit einem Angriffskrieg überzogen wird. Unsere Solidarität, unsere Unterstützung und unsere Hochachtung dafür sind Ihnen sicher. Und ich denke, ich spreche auch im Namen der deutschen Wirtschaft und uns aller hier, wenn ich sage: Die Ukraine kann sich auf Deutschland verlassen.\nSchönen Dank!\nBeitrag teilen\nper E-Mail teilen, announcement.", "limitations": ["The source describes commitments and announced policy, not complete 2023 implementation outcomes."], "start_unit": 8, "end_unit": 8, "locator": "extracted HTML block 8", "exact_excerpt": "(open in a new window)\nEPUB\n(open in a new window)\nOn February 27, 2022, just three days after Russia invaded Ukraine, Chancellor Olaf Scholz announced that this was a\nZeitenwende\n(“turning point”) for Germany, which would soon unveil a new foreign policy. He announced changes in Germany’s relations with Russia (harsh sanctions and ending Germany’s dependence on Russian energy supplies), support for Ukraine in the form of military equipment, and most importantly a shift in German defense policy. Scholz announced the creation of a 100-billion euro special fund to finance the modernization of Germany’s armed forces (the Bundeswehr), an increase in defense spending up to more than 2 percent of GDP per year, and the creation of a “powerful, ultra-modern, progressive Bundeswehr” equipped with “airplanes that fly, ships that can set out to sea, and soldiers who are optimally equipped for their missions.”\nFootnote\n1\nSince that speech in February 2022, Chancellor Scholz has developed an even more ambitious vision of Germany’s role in Europe—to be the “guarantor of European security,”\nFootnote\n2\ntaking “leading responsibility for the security of [the] continent,” and becoming “the cornerstone of conventional defense in Europe.”\nFootnote\n3\nIs this vision realistic? Despite initial optimism, today many people in the foreign policy community are skeptical about whether Germany will really be able to turn its defense policy around.\nFootnote\n4\nThey see Germany’s strategic culture as the main reason for its military restraint.\nFootnote\n5\nAlthough I share the view that Germany will continue its policy of military restraint, I offer an alternative explanation. Germany’s defense policy is already falling short of expectations, and in the future will not fulfill the promises created by Chancellor Scholz. In my view, it is not its strategic culture that encourages Germany to pursue a restrained defense policy; it is the structure of the international system. I argue that Germany has strong incentives from a realist point of view to keep its military spending moderate.\nFootnote\n6\nThis conclusion rests on the idea that a state dealing with an aggressor has two principal strategies to choose from: balancing or buck-passing. Buck-passing is the preferable strategy because it allows the state in question to avoid confronting a threat or shouldering the burden of such a confrontation. A state following a buck-passing strategy contributes as little as required to the alliance, relying instead mainly on the efforts of other members. It transfers as many of the costs of balancing as it can to its allies and increases its military posture only when those allies fail to catch the buck.\nFootnote", "excerpt_sha256": "89c07c8324f032321c7f3c8986b0219452e9becd1d6cf366ccd00dd6fed13f55", "verification_status": "accepted", "verification_notes": []}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0015-E003", "source_id": "SRC-f0ebd9566518186d", "url": "https://doi.org/10.1080/0163660X.2024.2398318", "title": "The Illusion of Germany’s Zeitenwende", "publisher": "The Washington Quarterly / Taylor & Francis", "raw_sha256": "ca920bc4a40f31b07b45fe0727be579483088eb3773fdbe7be58fb3970bb9ebe", "fact_summary": "Germany’s defense spending remained historically low relative to GDP before Scholz’s program: it fluctuated between 1.1% and 1.4% of GDP from 2005–2022, and most Bundeswehr weapons systems still had low operational readiness at the turn of 2021–2022; the 2023 Defense Policy Guidelines acknowledged decades of neglect and abandoned structures and capabilities needed for national and collective defense.", "question_ids": ["1B.3", "2B.8", "8B.10", "8B.7"], "polarity": "adverse", "period_fit": "The institutional finding and 2023 guidelines directly describe the inherited and continuing position during 2023.", "ruler_attribution": "The 2023 acknowledgment is issued by the Federal Ministry of Defence under Scholz’s government; earlier readiness failures predate his chancellorship.", "limitations": ["The source does not establish that Scholz personally caused the inherited deficiencies."], "start_unit": 12, "end_unit": 14, "locator": "extracted HTML block 12 through extracted HTML block 14", "exact_excerpt": "In the Defense Policy Guidelines published in 2003, international conflict prevention and crisis management shifted to the forefront of the Bundeswehr’s tasks, and defense spending was reduced.\nFootnote\n27\nThe Defense Policy Guidelines of 2011, written during the same peaceful environment, begins with the statement: “A direct territorial threat to Germany involving conventional military means remains an unlikely event.”\nFootnote\n28\nThe result was that, thanks to budget decreases since 1990, the Bundeswehr was poorly equipped and the operational readiness of most German weapon systems was low.\nFootnote\n29\nGermany’s strategic situation changed with the 2014 Ukraine crisis and Russia’s annexation of Crimea. Germany’s government has admitted that “Russia's aggressive actions against Ukraine have fundamentally challenged our vision of a Europe whole, free, and at peace.”\nFootnote\n30\nDuring NATO’s 2014 summit in Wales, Berlin agreed to raise its military spending up to 2 percent of GDP within a decade. In the White Book of 2016, more attention was paid to traditional security threats, and Germany voiced its aspirations of becoming a leader of European defense.\nFootnote\n31\nGermany’s strategic situation changed with the 2014 Ukraine crisis but it still buck-passed to the US\nBut instead of balancing Russia and strengthening the Bundeswehr after the first Ukraine crisis, Germany largely continued to pass the buck of defense on to the United States. The shift in German defense policy after 2014 proved to be mostly rhetorical; a disjuncture between words and deeds quickly emerged. Even the rhetoric was short-lived. As early as 2019, Chancellor Angela Merkel reduced the 2024 defense spending target of 2 percent of GDP to just 1.5 percent, and in that same year her defense minister Annegret Kramp-Karrenbauer announced that Germany would not achieve the 2 percent target until 2031.\nFootnote\n32\nDue to its low defense spending in relation to GDP between 1990 and 2020, Germany reaped a particularly big peace dividend. Whereas in 1990 it spent 2.5 percent of its GDP on defense, between 2005 and 2022, Germany’s defense spending fluctuated between 1.1 and 1.4 percent of GDP.\nFootnote\n33\nIf measured compared with the average levels of German defense spending in the 1980s, over the three decades Germany saved 394 billion euros. In comparison, within the same time frame, two other European powers in NATO, the United Kingdom and France, saved 111 billion and 25 billion euros, respectively.\nFootnote\n34\n\nEven taking into account all of the differences in these states’ strategy, this still gives a good idea of the magnitude of Germany’s defense cuts in the last 3 decades.\nBut since the early 1990s, Germany has also taken part in a variety of “out of area” missions. From 1991 to August 2017, the Bundeswehr participated in 52 operations. Germany’s Federal Constitutional Court confirmed in a 1994 decision that “out-of-area” missions conform with Germany’s Basic Law, and already at the beginning of the 21\nst\ncentury over 10,000 German soldiers were simultaneously deployed abroad.\nFootnote\n35\nNor is Germany a military dwarf. Despite the low level of military spending in relation to GDP, in absolute terms Germany has continued to be among the countries with the highest military expenditures throughout the post-Cold War period. It continuously ranks among the top ten military spenders in the world.\nFootnote\n36\nIts low ratio of defense spending to GDP only shows the low level of effort the state is devoting to its defense, but not necessarily the amount of military power it has. Thanks to Germany’s high GDP, even by spending a relatively small portion of its GDP, it is able to equip its army with modern weapon systems. Chancellor Gerhard Schröder (1998-2005) saw this as one of his main achievements: transforming Germany into a state in which military instruments are used cautiously but are not “taboo.”\nFootnote\n37\nBy 2022, the Bundeswehr was the fifth largest army in Europe (behind Russia, Ukraine, Turkey and France), and owned a relatively large number of modern weapon systems.\nFootnote\n38\nGermany also has a well-developed defense industry. It produces a wide range of weapons systems for land, air and sea, frequently in cooperation with European partners. Despite limited funds, Germany continues to produce and procure new weapons systems.\nFootnote\n39\nGerman defense companies have also successfully exported their products around the world; from 1990-2022, Germany was the fourth-largest arms exporter, behind the United States, Russia and France.\nFootnote\n40\nApart from Germany’s low level of defense spending in relation to GDP, the main challenge for the Bundeswehr has been how defense spending is structured. Germany has been spending relatively little on new armaments and maintenance, although this category of spending doubled in 2022 (19.48 billion euros) in comparison with 2010-2014 (8.75-10.13 billion euros). One of the most important investments in the last decade was to design a new generation of Eurofighter Typhoon jet fighters, under Tranche 4 of Project “Quadriga”. In 2020, 38 jet fighters were ordered; they will be delivered by 2030.\nFootnote\n41\nDespite a modest increase in military spending after 2014, most of the Bundeswehr’s weapons systems were still in a state of low operational readiness at the turn of 2021-22. Germany continues to depend on US security guarantees and continues to pass the defense buck on to the United States, a policy that has been regularly criticized by successive American administrations.\n\nFootnote\n42\nWhen the Ukrainian-Russian war began in 2022, the German Army was “more or less broke,” as posted on social media by the head of the Bundeswehr, Lt. Gen. Alfons Mais.\nFootnote\n43\nIn the Defense Policy Guidelines of 2023, it was acknowledged that the Bundeswehr had been “neglected” for decades, and that “common structures and capabilities necessary for national and collective defence were abandoned.”\nFootnote\n44\nWill Germany follow its own declarations this time, or forget them once again as we get further from Russia’s invasion of Ukraine?\nGermany’s\nZeitenwende\n? Or More of the Same?\nI anticipate that the four factors favoring buck-passing set out above will encourage Germany to continue to pass the buck on Europe’s defense to its allies. In fact, the first pieces of evidence that it is doing so have already appeared. Germany’s behavior demonstrates that it continues to prefer to rely on its allies for security.\nGeography\nRussia’s invasion of Ukraine did not change Germany’s strategic situation as dramatically as one might expect. Russia showed that it is not capable of becoming a regional hegemon. Germany still enjoys an advantageous geographical location, as it is not a NATO frontline state and remains surrounded by friends and allies, separated from Russia, Belarus and Ukraine by Poland and the Czech Republic. As indicated in Foreign Minister Annalena Baerbock’s preface to the National Security Strategy, Germany is “a strong country at the heart of Europe surrounded by partners.”\nFootnote\n45\nThe strategy further states that one of the factors shaping Germany’s security interests is its “geographic location.”\nFootnote\n46\nGerman authorities see Russia primarily as a regional threat to European order.\nFootnote\n47\nConsequently, Germany demonstrates support for defending its allies. It is increasing its military presence in the countries of NATO’s eastern flank: Lithuania, Slovakia and Poland. Germany has also deployed additional German Navy ships in the Baltic Sea.\nFootnote", "excerpt_sha256": "71306a5cb39d6a7251bc001caf286f011ff74c4e91f9619e04864ee16385b18d", "verification_status": "accepted", "verification_notes": []}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0015-E005", "source_id": "SRC-f0ebd9566518186d", "url": "https://doi.org/10.1080/0163660X.2024.2398318", "title": "The Illusion of Germany’s Zeitenwende", "publisher": "The Washington Quarterly / Taylor & Francis", "raw_sha256": "ca920bc4a40f31b07b45fe0727be579483088eb3773fdbe7be58fb3970bb9ebe", "fact_summary": "The €100 billion special fund and 2% defense-spending commitment were judged insufficient by the Bundestag Defense Committee chair, the parliamentary commissioner for the armed forces, and Defense Minister Pistorius, who reportedly estimated that about €300 billion was needed merely to remedy existing deficiencies; initial spending was slow and inflation, interest rates, and VAT reduced the fund’s real value.", "question_ids": ["2B.8", "8B.3", "8B.6", "8B.7"], "polarity": "adverse", "period_fit": "The evidence concerns the first year after the 2022 announcement and conditions documented in 2023.", "ruler_attribution": "The funding decision is attributed to Scholz’s government; the adequacy and implementation criticisms come from parliamentary and executive officials.", "limitations": ["The €300 billion estimate is an expert/political assessment, not a completed audited requirement."], "start_unit": 16, "end_unit": 17, "locator": "extracted HTML block 16 through extracted HTML block 17", "exact_excerpt": "55\nAnd although some see a threat to the United States’ European security umbrella if the administration were to change to one that is isolationist, the German government is showing no signs of panic, and for good reason.\nDespite the discontent shown by German elites toward President Donald Trump’s occasional criticism of NATO countries not investing 2 percent of their GDP in defense, in fact the number of US troops in Europe increased slightly under the Trump administration, Trump praised countries that were investing in defense, and the United States developed security cooperation with Ukraine.\nFootnote\n56\nTrump’s criticism of Germany and other European countries over their low defense spending and energy dependence on Russia did not really differ much from what had previously been expressed by his predecessors, whether Democrats or Republicans. And since February 2022, Germany has increased its defense spending and has ended its dependence on Russian energy supply; this should reduce any potential disputes with the next US administration, whoever leads it. The United States intends to continue its decades-long military commitment to Europe, and has the ability to do so. US-Russian relations are also deeply poisoned, which means the United States has little chance to defect from Europe.\nBut membership in NATO does not mean relying on US military guarantees only. Other NATO members have also increased their defense spending since the invasion of Ukraine. The most radical example is Poland, which increased its defense spending from 2.2 percent of GDP in 2022 up to 4.2 percent in 2024. Poland has also gone on a spending spree unprecedented in Europe, purchasing new weapons systems for its air, land, and sea forces.\nFootnote\n57\nThe number of German allies has also increased since 2022. In 2023 Finland joined NATO, and in 2024 Sweden followed it. There are also two European nuclear powers: France and the United Kingdom. Both are among the world’s top ten countries in terms of defense spending, and both are planning to increase it over the next decade.\nBeyond the US, other German allies such as Poland have increased their defense spending\nGermany must consider the role of nuclear weapons in its own defense policy. Like all NATO members, it benefits from the nuclear umbrella provided by the United States, along with the United Kingdom and France. Their nuclear arsenals are a powerful incentive for outside powers to think twice about attacking any NATO member. Germany has no such ability to deter, and does not plan to develop it. The German authorities reacted very cautiously to proposals by French President Emmanuel Macron on the role of French nuclear forces in Europe’s collective security.\nFootnote\n58\nGermany prefers to rely on its allies for nuclear deterrence. Due to its non-nuclear status, Germany is ill-suited to play the role of “guarantor of European security,” for its deterrence capabilities are less than those of the nuclear powers.\nThe High Cost of a Military Build-up\nA military build-up is enormously expensive, and its costs increase over time, so Germany would prefer to focus on civilian spending over defense spending. A pillar of the new defense policy has been the establishment of a special 100-billion euro fund to finance defense investments. According to Scholz, this special fund is the biggest turning point in German security policy since the founding of the Bundeswehr in 1955.\nFootnote\n59\nIt allowed Germany to achieve 2 percent GDP defense spending by 2024.\nFootnote\n60\nBut during the first year since\nZeitenwende\nwas declared, questions have mounted over whether establishing the special fund and increasing defense spending up to 2 percent of GDP will be enough to rebuild the Bundeswehr. The chairperson of the Defense Committee of the Bundestag, Marie-Agnes Strack-Zimmermann, the parliamentary commissioner for the armed forces Eva Högl, and Defense Minister Boris Pistorius all believe that not 100 billion euros, but 300 billion euros, are needed just to compensate for existing deficiencies in the Bundeswehr, not to mention more ambitious goals,\n\nFootnote\n61\nlike becoming a “guarantor of European security.”\nFootnote\n62\nInitially, the rate of spending was also low, which meant that the value of the special fund was actually decreasing because of rising interest rates, high inflation, and a required VAT deduction.\nFootnote\n63\nIn the 2023 National Security Strategy, the goal was already being watered down: “We will allocate two percent of our GDP, as an average over a multi-year period.”\nFootnote\n64\nAt the 2024 Munich Security Conference, Defense Minister Boris Pistorius stated: “I am proud to say that this year we will spend over 2 percent of our GDP on defense.” But he also publicly lobbied for more, stating “I am also realistic enough to see that this might not be enough in the years to come.”\nFootnote\n65\nHe even mentioned an increase in defense spending of up to 3.5 percent of GDP, but Chancellor Scholz and Finance Minister Christian Lindner oppose this.\nFootnote\n66\nMoreover, the information on spending published by the government has been questioned by German opposition politicians. Roderich Kiesewetter, a CDU party foreign and security policy expert, argues that aid for Ukraine is being financed through the special fund, which does not contribute to the long-term modernization of the Bundeswehr. He has accused the government of creating “a system of non-transparency, a system intended to give the impression that Germany can still get to the 2% target.”\nFootnote\n67\nMuch of the special fund will probably be invested in programs that had already been planned before\nZeitenwende\nwas announced but had not received appropriate funding.\nFootnote\n68", "excerpt_sha256": "c27012a0ec023120f6fb34e93a8dde98741853b09f6cdf1d52c5fec42f30054e", "verification_status": "accepted", "verification_notes": []}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0016-E006", "source_id": "SRC-ffa5a98c7207485e", "url": "https://www.bundesregierung.de/breg-en/service/archive/speech-by-chancellor-scholz-at-the-munich-security-conference-on-17-february-2023-in-munich-2166536", "title": "Speech by Chancellor Scholz at the Munich Security Conference on 17 February 2023 in Munich", "publisher": "Federal Government of Germany", "raw_sha256": "8277a801e1e91a012f5b0e3e13d617f0f1c91da6b650d0c9d4c462d11f339657", "fact_summary": "Scholz stated that Germany had created a €100 billion Bundeswehr special fund through an amendment to the Basic Law, intended to build military capability, and committed to permanently spending 2% of GDP on defence; Defence Minister Pistorius, Foreign Minister Baerbock and myself are canvassing intensively for this – and we will do so again here in Munich.\nGermany will do everything it can to make this decision easier for our partners. For instance, by training Ukrainian soldiers here in Germany or providing support in terms of supplies and logistics.\nBy the way, I see this as an example of the kind of leadership which everyone is entitled to expect from Germany – and I expressly offer it to our friends and partners.\nAnd that brings me to my fourth message: Germany is committed to living up to its responsibility for Europe’s security and that of NATO Allied territory – without any ifs or buts.\nIn previous years, it has often been said from this platform that Germany has to live up to its responsibility in the security policy sphere.\nI share this view – and we will indeed fulfil our responsibility.\nBy providing an additional brigade to protect Lithuania.\nBy assisting Poland and Slovakia with air defence and air policing.\nBy protecting critical infrastructure in the North and Baltic Seas.\nAnd by leading NATO’s Very High Readiness Joint Task Force and keeping 17,000 troops on standby to that end.\nIn order
